Several conditions can co-occur with autism at a higher rate than in the rest of the population.[^cite_note-Levy_2009-295] This may be referred to as [comorbidity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comorbidity), and may increase with age, causing difficulties for youth on the autism spectrum, and can make interventions and therapies more challenging. Features of autism and other diagnoses often overlap, and autism's characteristics can make traditional diagnostic procedures more difficult.[^cite_note-Underwood_2010-296][^cite_note-Helverschou_2011-297] This is sometimes known as [diagnostic overshadowing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diagnostic_overshadowing_in_autism).[^cite_note-298] Common co-occurring conditions are:

- [ADHD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ADHD) is seen in between 25% to 32% of autistic people.

  [^cite_note-MH-299]

  &#x20;Characteristics similar to those of ADHD can be part of an autism diagnosis.

  [^cite_note-Rommelse_et_al.-300]

- [Epilepsy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epilepsy) occurs in about 10% of autistic people.

  [^cite_note-301]

  &#x20;The risk is higher for older autistic people and those with intellectual disability.

  [^cite_note-302]

- [Intellectual disabilities](18567040.md) are some of the most common co-occurring conditions with autism (30% to 40%).

  [^cite_note-303]

  &#x20;As autism diagnosis is increasingly given to people with lower support needs, there is a tendency for the proportion with co-occurring intellectual disability to decrease over time.

- Various [anxiety disorders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anxiety_disorder) can co-occur with autism, with overall co-occurring rates of 17% to 23%.

  [^cite_note-MH-299]

  &#x20;Many anxiety disorders have characteristics better explained by autism itself, or are hard to distinguish from autism's features.

  [^cite_note-304]

- Rates of co-occurring [depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Depression_\(mood\)) in autistic people range from 9% to 13%.

  [^cite_note-MH-299]

- [Obsessive–compulsive disorder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obsessive%E2%80%93compulsive_disorder) (OCD) occurs in 7% to 10% of autistic people.

  [^cite_note-MH-299]

- Starting in adolescence, some autistic people fall under the criteria for the similar-looking [schizoid personality disorder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schizoid_personality_disorder), which is characterized by a [lack of interest in social relationships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asociality), a tendency toward a solitary or sheltered lifestyle, secretiveness, emotional coldness, detachment, and apathy.

  [^cite_note-305]

- [Genetic conditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_disorder): About 10% of autistic people are diagnosed with a rare genetic syndrome such as [Prader–Willi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prader%E2%80%93Willi_syndrome), [Angelman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angelman_syndrome), [Fragile X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fragile_X_syndrome), or [16p11.2 deletion syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16p11.2_deletion_syndrome).

  [^cite_note-306]

- [Gastrointestinal problems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gastrointestinal_disease) are among the most commonly reported co-occurring medical conditions in autistic people, and occur at higher rates than in the general population.

  [^cite_note-IsraelyanMargolis2018-307]

  [^cite_note-308]

  &#x20;Frequently reported issues include chronic constipation, diarrhea, abdominal pain, gastroesophageal reflux, and functional gastrointestinal disorders.

  [^cite_note-David_M_2021-309]

  &#x20;Gastrointestinal symptoms in autistic people have been associated with increased irritability, distress, sleep disturbances, and challenges in communication, particularly in children who have limited spoken language.

  [^cite_note-IsraelyanMargolis2018-307]

  [^cite_note-RaoGershon2016-310]

  [^cite_note-311]

  [^cite_note-312]

  &#x20;The underlying mechanisms are not fully understood and are likely multifactorial, involving interactions between diet, gut motility, sensory sensitivities, stress responses, and the gut-brain axis.

  [^cite_note-313]

  [^cite_note-David_M_2021-309]

- Sleep problems affect about two-thirds of autistic people at some point in childhood. These most commonly include symptoms of [insomnia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insomnia), such as difficulty falling asleep, frequent [nocturnal awakenings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nocturnal_awakenings), and early-morning awakenings. Sleep problems are associated with difficult behaviors and family stress, and are often a focus of clinical attention over and above the autism diagnosis.

  [^cite_note-314]

- Motor difficulties, including features of [dyspraxia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developmental_coordination_disorder), are highly prevalent in autistic people,

  [^cite_note-315]

  &#x20;and there is a significantly higher rate of [joint hypermobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypermobility_\(joints\))/[hypermobility spectrum disorders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypermobility_spectrum_disorder)/[Ehlers–Danlos syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ehlers%E2%80%93Danlos_syndrome) in autistic people.

  [^cite_note-316]

- There is tentative evidence that [gender dysphoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_dysphoria) occurs more frequently in autistic people.

  [^cite_note-317]

  [^cite_note-318]

### Prevalence

The World Health Organization notes that reports on prevalence vary widely. It estimates that about 1 in 127 children were autistic between 2012 and 2021 with a trend of increasing prevalence over time. This may reflect an underestimate of prevalence in [low-](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Least_developed_countries) and [middle-income countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newly_industrialized_country).[^cite_note-Autism-Research-3][^cite_note-World_Health_Organization-2022-4] Surveillance studies in community samples of adults suggest a similar share of the adult population would meet diagnostic criteria if formally assessed.[^cite_note-Hirota-2023-17] The number of people diagnosed has increased since the 1990s, likely due to a combination of increased recognition of autism, better availability of diagnosis, and changes to the diagnostic criteria.[^cite_note-Autism-Research-3] The increase in autism is largely attributable to changes in diagnostic practices, referral patterns, availability of services, age at diagnosis, and public awareness,[^cite_note-Fombonne-2009-319][^cite_note-320][^cite_note-321] particularly among women,[^cite_note-322] though other unidentified environmental factors may exist.[^cite_note-Rut2005-323]

The [Centers for Disease Control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centers_for_Disease_Control)'s Autism and Developmental Disabilities Monitoring Network reported that approximately 1 in 31 children in the United States is diagnosed with autism, based on data collected in 2022.[^cite_note-DS-324] For 2016 data, the estimate was 1 in 54, compared to 1 in 68 in 2010 and 1 in 150 in 2000.[^cite_note-DS-324] Diagnostic criteria for autism have changed significantly since the 1980s; for example, [U.S. special-education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_education_in_the_United_States) autism classification was introduced in 1994.[^cite_note-Newschaffer_2007-325]

### Sex ratio

Males are about three times more likely to be diagnosed with autism than females.[^cite_note-Loomes-2017-22] [Several theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_differences_in_autism) about the higher prevalence in males have been investigated.[^cite_note-Chaste-326] Prevalence differences may be mainly due to a result of differences in expression of characteristics between males and females, with [autistic women and girls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_and_gender_differences_in_autism) showing less atypical behaviors and therefore being less likely to be diagnosed with autism.[^cite_note-327]

### Sexuality and gender identity

Main article: [Autism and LGBTQ people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autism_and_LGBTQ_people)

The common belief that most autistic people are not interested in sexual or romantic relationships, which largely formed due to older studies with poor methodology, has been refuted by more recent studies.[^cite_note-\:6-328][^cite_note-329] However, both autistic men and autistic women are more likely to be [asexual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asexuality) than their non-autistic counterparts.[^cite_note-\:7-330]

Research indicates that autistic people are significantly more likely to be [LGBTQ](78174325.md) than the general population.[^cite_note-\:5-24][^cite_note-Graham_Holmes-2022-26] More specifically, studies show they are less likely to be [heterosexual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterosexual) and more likely to be [transgender](19904525.md) or [gender-nonconforming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender-nonconforming), with [gender dysphoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_dysphoria) also being more common.[^cite_note-Graham_Holmes-2022-26][^cite_note-\:6-328] One study from 2018 found about 70% of its autistic participants identified as non-heterosexual compared to only about 30% of the non-autistic group.[^cite_note-\:6-328] The range of sexual orientations among autistic people has also been found to be more diverse than among non-autistic people.[^cite_note-\:7-330]

## History

This section is an excerpt from [History of autism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_autism).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=History_of_autism\&action=edit)]

The [history of autism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_autism) encompasses various conceptual and treatment approaches, with the understanding of autism having been shaped by cultural, scientific, and societal factors.[^cite_note-History_of_autism_\:66-331] Pathologized or viewed as beneficial as part of [neurodiversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neurodiversity), autism has been subject to various treatments (e.g. electroconvulsive therapy)[^cite_note-History_of_autism_\:53-332][^cite_note-333] Progress in scientific understanding of autism has contributed to both treatment and perceptions of autism.[^cite_note-History_of_autism_\:66-331]

### Etymology

In 1911, Swiss psychiatrist [Paul Eugen Bleuler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eugen_Bleuler) [coined](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neologism) the German term *Autismus* to characterize the social withdrawal he observed in people with [schizophrenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schizophrenia).[^cite_note-334] Rendered in English as *autism*, the term derives from the Greek word [autos](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/auto) ("self").\[^cite_note-[www.britannica.com-2025-335](http://www.britannica.com-2025-335/)] Bleuler used the term for a symptom of adult schizophrenia: a person's retreat from reality into their own subjective world. He also mentioned "autistic thinking" as a fleeting, illogical thought process everyone experiences. In the mid-1920s, German psychiatrist [Fritz Künkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fritz_K%C3%BCnkel) categorized over 100 schizophrenic patients into four groups, one of which he labeled "autistic", highlighting disturbances in emotional life. In 1926, building on his work and Bleuler's, [Grunya Sukhareva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grunya_Sukhareva) published an article about six boys who were musically gifted and had a tendency toward abstract thinking. She called their affects "flattened" and their tendency to avoid other children an "autistic attitude".[^cite_note-336] Scholars have credited Sukhareva with making observations that closely mirror ASD as described by the [DSM-5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DSM-5) and [ICD-11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICD-11).[^cite_note-Sher-2023-337] Her work expanded on the definition throughout her career while making great strides in differentiating ASD and schizophrenia nearly 30 years before the establishment of separate classifications for these diagnoses with the 1980 publication of the [DSM-III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DSM-III).[^cite_note-338]

## Society and culture

Main article: [Societal and cultural aspects of autism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Societal_and_cultural_aspects_of_autism)

Greta Thunberg has balayage hair, she smiles.

In 2021, Swedish climate activist [Greta Thunberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greta_Thunberg) likened her autism to a "superpower", crediting her success to her [focused interests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_interest_\(autism\)).

[^cite_note-Hattenstone-2021-339]

The [autistic rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autism_rights_movement) and [neurodiversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neurodiversity) movements argue autism should be accepted as a difference to be accommodated instead of cured,[^cite_note-340][^cite_note-341][^cite_note-Solomon-342][^cite_note-343][^cite_note-Silverman_2008-344] although a minority of autistic people might still accept a cure.[^cite_note-345] Social-science scholars study autistic people in hopes of learning more about "autism as a culture, transcultural comparisons ... and research on social movements".[^cite_note-Silverman_2008-344] Events related to autism include [World Autism Awareness Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Autism_Awareness_Day), [Autism Sunday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autism_Sunday), [Autistic Pride Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autistic_Pride_Day), [Autreat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autism_Network_International), and others.[^cite_note-346][^cite_note-347][^cite_note-348][^cite_note-349]

[Focused interests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_interest_\(autism\)) are commonly found in autistic people and can include activism. Environmental activist [Greta Thunberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greta_Thunberg) has spoken favorably about her autism diagnosis, saying that autism can be a source of [life purpose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_purpose), as well as the basis of careers, hobbies, and friendships.[^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351][^cite_note-Hattenstone-2021-339] Entrepreneur and co-founder of [Microsoft](19001.md) [Bill Gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Gates) has written, "If I were growing up today, I probably would be diagnosed on the autism spectrum."[^cite_note-352]

### Legal implications of diagnosis

In Sweden, people with an autism diagnosis are required to provide additional proof of suitability to apply for a driver's license[^cite_note-Autism_Sverige-353] and may face [barriers to entering military service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_service_and_conscription_for_autistic_people).\[^cite_note-[www.do.se-354](http://www.do.se-354/)]

In the United Kingdom, an autistic police officer was rejected from firearms training, which a tribunal later ruled was illegal discrimination.[^cite_note-Rowsell-2023-355]

In the United States, autism is generally a "disqualifying condition" for joining the military, but autistic people can apply for a medical waiver.[^cite_note-356]

### Discrimination

[Discrimination against autistic people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discrimination_against_autistic_people) occurs in various forms, both directly and indirectly, in a variety of settings, professional, educational, or clinical.[^cite_note-Cleary-2023-357] Such discrimination is often initiated after other people identify the autistic person as different from themselves, which may occur very quickly.[^cite_note-Cleary-2023-357] This discrimination sometimes leads to [violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violence), [assault](1466.md), or [social exclusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_exclusion).[^cite_note-Cleary-2023-357] Various legal limitations on people with an autism diagnosis have also been considered discriminatory.[^cite_note-Autism_Sverige-353][^cite_note-Rowsell-2023-355]

### Neurodiversity movement



Flag representing the [autistic rights movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autistic_rights_movement). The color scheme is influenced by the [pride flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pride_flag), but missing the color blue, as specific campaigns using the color have been denounced by the autism community.

[^cite_note-358]

&#x20;The middle band is inspired by the elemental symbol of [gold](12240.md) (Au).

[^cite_note-359]

&#x20;The [infinity sign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infinity_symbol) is a symbol long used in the wider autism and [neurodiversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neurodiversity) movement.

[^cite_note-360]

Some autistic people and affiliated researchers[^cite_note-Pellicano-361] have advocated a shift in attitudes toward the view that autism is a difference, rather than a disease that ought to be treated or cured.[^cite_note-362][^cite_note-363][^cite_note-364] Critics have bemoaned the entrenchment of some of these groups' opinions, and that they speak to a select group of autistic people with limited difficulties.[^cite_note-Solomon-342][^cite_note-Morgan-2016-365][^cite_note-Silverman_2008-344][^cite_note-366][^cite_note-HV-367] Clinical and policy guidance in Australia, the United States, and the United Kingdom now promote [neurodiversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neurodiversity)-affirming language—for example, using "characteristics" instead of "symptoms" and avoiding words such as "cure".[^cite_note-368][^cite_note-England-218][^cite_note-369]

The neurodiversity movement and the [autism rights movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autism_rights_movement) are [social movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_movement) within the context of [disability rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disability_rights), emphasizing the concept of neurodiversity, according to which autism is a result of healthy and valuable variations in the [human brain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_brain) rather than a disorder to be cured.[^cite_note-Solomon-342][^cite_note-The_Guardian-370] The autism rights movement advocates including greater acceptance of autistic behaviors, therapies that focus on coping skills rather than [imitating the behaviors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autistic_masking) of non-autistic people,[^cite_note-Bigthink-371] and the recognition of the autistic community as a [minority group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_group).[^cite_note-Bigthink-371][^cite_note-HV-367] Autism rights or neurodiversity advocates believe the autism spectrum is genetic and should be accepted as a healthy variation in the [human genome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_genome).[^cite_note-Solomon-342] A common argument against neurodiversity activists is that most have relatively few support needs or are [self-diagnosed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-diagnosed), and do not represent the views of autistic people with more support needs.[^cite_note-HV-367][^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373] It has been argued that only autistic people with lower support needs should be included under the neurodiversity banner, as autism with high support needs "may rightfully be viewed as a disability".[^cite_note-HV-367] The concept of neurodiversity is contentious in autism advocacy and research groups and has led to infighting.[^cite_note-Morgan-2016-365][^cite_note-Silverman_2008-344]

### Driving

Many autistic teenagers and adults struggle to learn to drive a car.[^cite_note-\:2-374] By age 21, about 20% of autistic people in the US have a [driver's license](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Driver's_license), compared to more than 60% of non-autistic people.[^cite_note-\:3-375] They are less likely to start learning, likely to do it later than non-autistic peers, and less likely to proceed to the next stage.[^cite_note-\:3-375]

The differences are due to the characteristics of autism such as being slower to recognize [pedestrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedestrians),[^cite_note-\:4-376] having a slower [reaction time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reaction_time),[^cite_note-\:4-376][^cite_note-\:2-374] worse [executive function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_function),[^cite_note-\:2-374] worse [motor coordination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motor_coordination),[^cite_note-\:2-374] and poorer [situational awareness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Situational_awareness) (e.g., remembering that another vehicle is in their [blind spot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vehicle_blind_spot)).[^cite_note-\:4-376] Autistic drivers report difficulty with [motor control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motor_control),[^cite_note-\:2-374] [sensory processing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sensory_processing) (e.g., the [glare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glare_\(vision\)) of oncoming headlights),[^cite_note-\:2-374] [visual-motor coordination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visual-motor_coordination),[^cite_note-\:2-374] [mental inflexibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rigidity_\(psychology\)) (e.g., failing to yield the [right of way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traffic#Passage_priority_\(right_of_way\))),[^cite_note-\:2-374] and difficulty with social cues (e.g., understanding how to communicate and cooperate with other drivers to navigate a road hazard).[^cite_note-\:2-374]

Although these problems mean that most autistic people do not learn how to drive, those autistic people who have earned driver's licenses have about the same chance as anyone else of being involved in a traffic collision and a lower risk of some problems, including driving at an [unsafe speed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speeding) or having their license suspended.[^cite_note-\:2-374]

### Parental reactions to a child's diagnosis

Parents' reactions to having their children diagnosed with autism vary. Many experience grief.[^cite_note-\:12-377] A study in Spain found that children diagnosed with autism and their parents faced stigma and societal challenges.[^cite_note-\:12-377] Stigma is created by societal standards that influence how parents think of autism.[^cite_note-378] Cultural values can influence how a parent feels; many feel defeated because they think they failed as a parent.[^cite_note-\:12-377] Spain has early intervention programs to help parents adapt to their children's diagnoses.[^cite_note-\:12-377] Stigma causes many parents to hide their children's condition, so their experiences are minimized or denied.[^cite_note-\:12-377]

### Diagnosis in adulthood

Since the 1990s, the number of people without co-occurring intellectual disability who are diagnosed with autism (including Asperger's syndrome and ASD) has increased. Some autistic adults of average or above-average intelligence were not diagnosed in childhood because the condition was less well-understood. Diagnosis in adulthood can have effects very different from those experienced by people diagnosed in childhood. A late diagnosis produces an emotional reaction and a phase of readjustment. Late-diagnosed adults often report a history of being treated for anxiety and depression. Many report lifelong feelings of isolation and alienation. After a period of readjustment, a late diagnosis is often viewed favorably, allowing previously unidentified needs to be met and support and accommodations to be requested.[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380][^cite_note-381]

[^cite_note-200]: &#x20;Grzadzinski R, Huerta M, Lord C (15 May 2013). ["DSM-5 and autism spectrum disorders (ASDs): an opportunity for identifying ASD subtypes"](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3671160). *Molecular Autism*. **4** (1): 12. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1186/2040-2392-4-12](https://doi.org/10.1186%2F2040-2392-4-12). [PMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMC_\(identifier\)) [3671160](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3671160). [PMID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMID_\(identifier\)) [23675638](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23675638). Consequently, the fifth edition of the DSM (DSM-5) replaces the multi-categorical system with a single diagnostic dimension: ASD.

[^cite_note-Lai-2013-201]: &#x20;Lai MC, Lombardo MV, Chakrabarti B, Baron-Cohen S (23 April 2013). ["Subgrouping the Autism 'Spectrum': Reflections on DSM-5"](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3635864). *PLOS Biology*. **11** (4) e1001544. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1371/journal.pbio.1001544](https://doi.org/10.1371%2Fjournal.pbio.1001544). [PMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMC_\(identifier\)) [3635864](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3635864). [PMID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMID_\(identifier\)) [23630456](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23630456). New in DSM-5 is the explicit recognition of the "spectrum" nature of autism, subsuming and replacing the DSM-IV Pervasive Developmental Disorder (PDD) categorical subgroups of "autistic disorder," "Asperger's disorder," "pervasive developmental disorder not otherwise specified," and "childhood disintegrative disorder" into a single umbrella term "Autism Spectrum Disorder" (ASD).

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDSM-5-TR"Autism_spectrum_disorder_encompasses_disorders_previously_referred_to_as_early_infantile_autism,_childhood_autism,_Kanner's_autism,_\[\[high-functioning_autism\]\],_\[\[atypical_autism\]\],_pervasive_developmental_disorder_not_otherwise_specified,_childhood_disintegrative_disorder,_and_Asperger's_disorder"-202]: &#x20;[DSM-5-TR](#CITEREFDSM-5-TR), "Autism spectrum disorder encompasses disorders previously referred to as early infantile autism, childhood autism, Kanner's autism, [high-functioning autism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-functioning_autism), [atypical autism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atypical_autism), pervasive developmental disorder not otherwise specified, childhood disintegrative disorder, and Asperger's disorder".

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDSM-5-TR"It_may_be_helpful_to_note_level_of_support_needed_for_each_of_the_two_core_psychopathological_domains_in_Table_2_(e.g.,_'requiring_very_substantial_support_for_deficits_in_social_communication_and_requiring_substantial_support_for_restricted,_repetitive_behaviors')"-203]: &#x20;[DSM-5-TR](#CITEREFDSM-5-TR), "It may be helpful to note level of support needed for each of the two core psychopathological domains in Table 2 (e.g., 'requiring very substantial support for deficits in social communication and requiring substantial support for restricted, repetitive behaviors')".

[^cite_note-US_screening-214]: &#x20;For example: US Preventive Services Task Force does not recommend universal screen of young children for autism due to poor evidence of benefits of this screening when parents and clinicians have no concerns about ASD. The major concern is a false-positive diagnosis that would burden a family with very time-consuming and financially demanding treatment interventions when it is not truly required. The Task Force also did not find any robust studies showing effectiveness of behavioral therapies in reducing ASD symptom severity.<sup>[\[210\]](#cite_note-Diagnosis_of_autism_Siu_2016-212)</sup> American Academy of Pediatrics recommends ASD screening of all children between the ages if 18 and 24 months.<sup>[\[210\]](#cite_note-Diagnosis_of_autism_Siu_2016-212)</sup> The AAP also recommends that children who screen positive for ASD be referred to treatment services without waiting for a comprehensive diagnostic workup<sup>[\[211\]](#cite_note-Diagnosis_of_autism_Blumberg_2016-213)</sup> The American Academy of Family Physicians did not find sufficient evidence of benefit of universal early screening for ASD<sup>[\[210\]](#cite_note-Diagnosis_of_autism_Siu_2016-212)</sup> The American Academy of Neurology and Child Neurology Society recommends general routine screening for delayed or abnormal development in children followed by screening for ASD only if indicated by the general developmental screening<sup>[\[210\]](#cite_note-Diagnosis_of_autism_Siu_2016-212)</sup> The American Academy of Child and Adolescent Psychiatry recommend routinely screening autism symptoms in young children<sup>[\[210\]](#cite_note-Diagnosis_of_autism_Siu_2016-212)</sup>

[^cite_note-GogouKolios2018-272]: &#x20;Gogou M, Kolios G (June 2018). "Are therapeutic diets an emerging additional choice in autism spectrum disorder management?". *World Journal of Pediatrics* (Review). **14** (3): 215–223. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1007/s12519-018-0164-4](https://doi.org/10.1007%2Fs12519-018-0164-4). [PMID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMID_\(identifier\)) [29846886](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29846886). Current literature knowledge provides evidence that ketogenic and casein/gluten-free diet may have their own place in our reserve for the therapeutic management of specific subsets of children with autism. ... More clinical studies about the effect of gluten/caseinfree diet in these patients are available. However, available data arise from studies with small sample size and are still controversial. In general, despite encouraging data, no definite proof still exists. Under this view, the use of therapeutic diets in children with autism should be restricted to specific subgroups, such as children with autism and epilepsy or specific inborn errors of metabolism *(ketogenic diet)*, children with known food intolerance/allergy or even children with food intolerance markers (*gluten- and casein-free diet*). Their implementation should always be guided by health care practitioners.
